Source: Statistical Yearbook of Jerusalem, 2021.[^cite_note-217] Totals do not sum exactly due to the presentation of some ethnoreligious groups as percentages of totals. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

In December 2007 Jerusalem had a population of 747,600—63.7% were Jewish, 33.1% Muslim, and 2% Christian.[^cite_note-218]

According to a study published in 2000, the percentage of Jews in the city's population had been decreasing; this was attributed to a higher Muslim [birth rate](515898.md), and Jewish residents leaving. The study also found that about nine percent of the Old City's 32,488 people were Jews.[^cite_note-219] Of the Jewish population, 200,000 live in East Jerusalem settlements which are considered illegal under international law.[^cite_note-220]

In 2005, 2,850 new immigrants settled in Jerusalem, mostly from the United States, France and the former [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). In terms of the local population, the number of outgoing residents exceeds the number of incoming residents. In 2005, 16,000 left Jerusalem and only 10,000 moved in.[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221] Nevertheless, the population of Jerusalem continues to rise due to the high birth rate, especially in the [Haredi Jewish](53835.md) and [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_people) communities. Consequently, the [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) in Jerusalem (4.02) is higher than in Tel Aviv (1.98) and well above the national average of 2.90. The average size of Jerusalem's 180,000 households is 3.8 people.[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221]

In 2005 the total population grew by 13,000 (1.8%)—similar to the Israeli national average, but the religious and ethnic composition is shifting. While 31% of the Jewish population is made up of children below the age fifteen, the figure for the Arab population is 42%.[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221]

In 1967 Jews accounted for 74% of the population, while the figure for 2006 is down by 9%.[^cite_note-222] Possible factors are the high cost of housing, fewer job opportunities and the increasingly religious character of the city, although proportionally, young [Haredim](53835.md) are leaving in higher numbers. The percentage of secular Jews, or those who 'wear their faith lightly' is dropping, with some 20,000 leaving the city over the past seven years (2012). They now number 31% of the population, the same percentage as the rising Haredi population.

In 2010, 61% of all Jewish children in Jerusalem studied in Haredi (Ultra-Orthodox) schools. This correlates with the high number of children in Haredi families.[^cite_note-223]

While some secular Jews leave Jerusalem for its relative lack of development and religious and political tensions, Jerusalem-born Palestinians cannot leave Jerusalem, or they lose their right to live in the city. Palestinians with a "Jerusalem resident status" are entitled to the subsidised healthcare and social security benefits Israel provides to its citizens, and have the right to vote in municipal elections, but not to be voted in municipal elections, or to vote in national elections. Arabs in Jerusalem can send their children to Israeli-run schools, although not every neighbourhood has one, and universities. Israeli doctors and highly regarded hospitals such as [Hadassah Medical Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadassah_Medical_Center) are available to residents.[^cite_note-224]

Demographics and the Jewish-Arab population divide play a major role in the dispute over Jerusalem. In 1998, the [Jerusalem Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Development_Authority) expanded city limits to the west to include more areas heavily populated with Jews.[^cite_note-Laub-2006-24]

&#x20;Within the past few years, there has been a steady increase in the Jewish birthrate and a steady decrease in the Arab birthrate. In May 2012 it was reported that the Jewish birthrate had overtaken the Arab birthrate. The city's birthrate stands about 4.2 children per Jewish family and 3.9 children per Arab family.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226] In addition, increasing numbers of Jewish immigrants chose to settle in Jerusalem. In the last few years, thousands of Palestinians have moved to previously fully Jewish neighbourhoods of East Jerusalem, built after the 1967 Six-Day War. In 2007, 1,300 Palestinians lived in the previously exclusively Jewish neighbourhood of [Pisgat Ze'ev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pisgat_Ze'ev) and constituted 3% of the population in [Neve Ya'akov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neve_Ya'akov). In the [French Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Hill_\(neighborhood\)) neighbourhood Palestinians today constitute one-sixth of the overall population.[^cite_note-227]



[Sheikh Jarrah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Jarrah), a predominantly Arab neighbourhood on the road to [Mount Scopus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Scopus)

At the end of 2008 the population of East Jerusalem was 456,300, comprising 60% of Jerusalem's residents. Of these 195,500 (43%) were Jews, (comprising 40% of the Jewish population of Jerusalem as a whole), and 260,800 (57%) were Muslim (comprising 98% of the Muslim population of Jerusalem).[^cite_note-228] In 2008 the [Palestinian Central Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Central_Bureau_of_Statistics) reported the number of Palestinians living in East Jerusalem was 208,000 according to a recently completed census.[^cite_note-229]

Jerusalem's Jewish population is overwhelmingly religious. Only 18% of Jewish residents are secular. In addition, [Haredi Jews](53835.md) comprise 35% of the city's adult Jewish population. In a phenomenon seen rarely around the world, the percentage of Jewish women who work, 81%, exceeds the percentage of Jewish men who work, 70%.[^cite_note-230]

Jerusalem had a population of 804,400 in 2011, of which Jews comprised 499,400 (62.1%), Muslims 281,100 (34.9%), Christians 14,700 (1.8%), and 9,000 (1.1%) were not classified by religion.[^cite_note-jerusaleminstitute.org.il-2022-26]

Jerusalem had a population of 882,700 in 2016, of which Jews comprised 536,600 (60.8%), Muslims 319,800 (36.2%), Christians 15,800 (1.8%), and 10,300 unclassified (1.2%).[^cite_note-jerusaleminstitute.org.il-2022-26]

Jerusalem had a population of 951,100 in 2020, of which [Jews](25955086.md) comprised 570,100 (59.9%), [Muslims](19541.md) 353.800 (37.2%), [Christians](18337522.md) 16.300 (1.7%), and 10,800 unclassified (1.1%).[^cite_note-jerusaleminstitute.org.il-2022-26]

&#x20;According to [Peace Now](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Now), approvals for building in Israeli settlements in [East Jerusalem](343508.md) expanded by 60% under [Donald Trump's first US presidency](52231341.md).[^cite_note-231] Since 1991, Palestinians, who make up the majority of the residents in East Jerusalem, have only received 30% of the building permits.[^cite_note-232]



Sign in [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language) in the [Armenian Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Quarter)

### Urban planning issues

Critics of efforts to promote a Jewish majority in Jerusalem say that government planning policies are motivated by demographic considerations and seek to limit Arab construction while promoting Jewish construction.[^cite_note-233] According to a [World Bank](36752.md) report, the number of recorded building violations between 1996 and 2000 was four and half times higher in Jewish neighbourhoods but four times fewer demolition orders were issued in West Jerusalem than in East Jerusalem; Arabs in Jerusalem were less likely to receive construction permits than Jews, and "the authorities are much more likely to take action against Palestinian violators" than Jewish violators of the permit process.[^cite_note-Movement_and_Access_Restrictions_in_the_West_Bank-234] In recent years, private Jewish foundations have received permission from the government to develop projects on disputed lands, such as the [City of David archaeological site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_David_\(archaeological_site\)) in the 60% Arab neighbourhood of [Silwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silwan) (adjacent to the Old City),[^cite_note-235] and the [Museum of Tolerance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Tolerance_Jerusalem) on Mamilla Cemetery (adjacent to Zion Square).[^cite_note-Movement_and_Access_Restrictions_in_the_West_Bank-234][^cite_note-236]

## Religious significance

Main article: [Religious significance of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_significance_of_Jerusalem)



The [Old City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City_\(Jerusalem\)) is home to many sites of seminal [religious importance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_significance_of_Jerusalem) for the three major [Abrahamic religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions)—[Judaism](15624.md), [Christianity](5211.md), and [Islam](6037917.md).

Jerusalem has been sacred to Judaism for roughly 3000 years, to Christianity for around 2000 years, and to Islam for approximately 1400 years. The 2000 Statistical Yearbook of Jerusalem lists 1204 synagogues, 158 churches, and 73 mosques within the city.[^cite_note-237] Despite efforts to maintain peaceful religious coexistence, some sites, such as the Temple Mount, have been a continuous source of friction and controversy. The [Temple Mount](65719.md) is the holiest spot in [Judaism](15624.md) and the third holiest site in Islam. Jews venerate it as the site of the two former [Temples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem) and [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) believe that [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam) was transported from the [Great Mosque of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Mecca) to this location during the [Night Journey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra_and_Mi'raj).

### Judaism

Further information: [Jerusalem in Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_in_Judaism)

Jerusalem has been the [holiest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Holy_Cities) in Judaism and the ancestral and spiritual homeland of the Jewish people since King David proclaimed it his capital in the 10th century BCE.[^cite_note-bible-david-28][^cite_note-Since_the_10th_century_BCE-31] Without counting its other names, Jerusalem appears in the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) 669 times.[^cite_note-238] The first five books of the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah) (Pentateuch), only mentions [Moriah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moriah), but in the rest of the Bible, the city is mentioned explicitly.[^cite_note-239] The Temple Mount, which was the site of [Solomon's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple) and the Second Temple, is the holiest site in Judaism and the place Jews throughout the world turn towards during prayer.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] The Western Wall, a remnant of the wall surrounding the Second Temple, attracts over 10 million visitors each year.[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] Synagogues around the world are traditionally built with the Holy Ark facing Jerusalem,[^cite_note-244] and Arks within Jerusalem face the [Holy of Holies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_of_Holies).[^cite_note-Segal-1987-245] As prescribed in the [Mishnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishnah) and codified in the *[Shulchan Aruch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulchan_Aruch)*, daily prayers are recited while facing towards Israel, Jerusalem and the Temple Mount;[^cite_note-246] The [Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud) cites [1 Kings 8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Kings_8) in ruling that the core daily prayers are recited facing towards Israel by those outside it,[^cite_note-247] while those reciting the prayer in Israel face Jerusalem and those in Jerusalem face towards the site of the [Holy of Holies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_of_Holies) on the Temple Mount.[^cite_note-248] Many Jews located west of Jerusalem have "[Mizrach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizrah)" plaques hung on a wall of their homes to indicate the direction of prayer to the east.[^cite_note-Segal-1987-245][^cite_note-250] The words "[L'Shana Haba'ah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Shana_Haba'ah)"(meaning "Next Year in Jerusalem") are traditionally recited at the conclusion of the [Yom Kippur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur) service and of the [Passover Seder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover_Seder) outside of Jerusalem. The passage first appears as part of the liturgy for Yom Kippur in the *[Machzor Vitry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machzor_Vitry)* published in the 12th-13th centuries, and for Passover in the *[Birds' Head Haggadah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birds'_Head_Haggadah)* published around 1300 and was attested a century later by Rabbi [Isaac Tyrnau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Tyrnau) as an accepted component of the Seder.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252] In the [Amidah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amidah) prayer, which is recited three times daily as the core element of [Jewish religious service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_prayer) and whose structure and blessings were formulated to replace [sacrificial rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korban) following the destruction of the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple), the fourteenth blessing pleads for [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism) to re-establish the [Davidic monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davidic_line) and return his [Divine presence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_presence) to a rebuilt Jerusalem.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] The third blessing of the [Grace After Meals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkat_Hamazon) asks God to speedily rebuild the holy city of Jerusalem, in a text which the [Babylonian Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonian_Talmud) ascribes to [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David) and [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon).[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] [The Three Weeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Three_Weeks) is an annual period of communal sorrow, which ends on the fast day of [Tisha B'Av](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisha_B'Av)—described as "the saddest day in the Jewish calendar"—which is dedicated to remembering and mourning the destruction in Jerusalem of [Solomon's Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon's_Temple) in 586 BCE and of the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple) in 70 CE.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260][^cite_note-261] A tradition dating back to the [Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud) ([Bava Batra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bava_Batra) 60b) requires that a patch of wall in a new home near the front door and measuring approximately 18 by 18 inches (46 by 46 cm) be left unfinished or unpainted as a perpetual reminder and a sign of mourning for the destruction of the Temple.[^cite_note-262]

#### Jerusalem remembered at weddings and mourning

Verses 5 and 6 of [Psalm 137](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psalm_137) and the words "If I forget thee O Jerusalem..." are customarily recited during a [Jewish wedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_wedding) ceremony shortly before breaking a glass as a symbolic act of mourning over the destruction of the Temple, even when at a time of greatest joy.[^cite_note-263]

There are various customs as to what to say when taking leave of the mourner(s). [Ashkenazi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashkenazi_Jews) recite a traditional phrase that wishes comfort to a mourner with a reassurance that they will eventually reconnect to the person who died, in the same way that [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism) comforts the Jewish people for the [destruction of the Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_CE\)) nearly 2,000 years ago through the promise of its eventual rebuilding in the [times of the Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism).[^cite_note-Mourning-264][^cite_note-265] The visitor says to the mourner:[^cite_note-Mourning-264]

הַמָּקוֹם יְנַחֵם אֶתְכֶם בְּתוֹךְ שְׁאָר אֲבֵלֵי צִיּוֹן וִירוּשָׁלָיִם

*Hamakom y'nachem etkhem b'tokh sha'ar avelei tziyon viyrushalayim*:

"May [The Omnipresent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_God_in_Judaism) comfort you (pl.) among the mourners of [Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zion) and Jerusalem"

### Christianity

Further information: [Jerusalem in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_in_Christianity)

Jerusalem is generally considered the cradle of Christianity.[^cite_note-266] Christianity reveres Jerusalem for its [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament) history, and also for its significance in the life of Jesus. According to the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), Jesus was brought to Jerusalem soon after his birth[^cite_note-267] and later in his life cleansed the Second Temple.[^cite_note-268] The [Cenacle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cenacle), believed to be the site of Jesus' [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper), is located on [Mount Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Zion) in the same building that houses the [Tomb of King David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David's_Tomb).[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] Another prominent Christian site in Jerusalem is [Golgotha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvary), the site of the [crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion). The [Gospel of John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_John) describes it as being located outside Jerusalem,[^cite_note-271] but recent archaeological evidence suggests Golgotha is a short distance from the Old City walls, within the present-day confines of the city.[^cite_note-Stump-1993-272] The land occupied by the [Church of the Holy Sepulchre](7810.md) is considered one of the top candidates for Golgotha and thus has been a Christian pilgrimage site for the past 2000 years.[^cite_note-Stump-1993-272][^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] The Church of the Holy Sepulchre is generally considered the most important church in [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom).[^cite_note-275] It contains the two holiest sites in [Christianity](5211.md): the site where [Jesus](1095706.md) was [crucified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus), and Jesus's empty tomb, where he is believed by Christians to have been [buried](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burial_of_Jesus) and [resurrected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus).

### Islam

Further information: [Jerusalem in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_in_Islam)

See also: [Islamization of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization_of_Jerusalem)

Jerusalem is the third-holiest city in [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam).[^cite_note-Esposito-2002-38] Islamic tradition holds that for approximately a year, before it was permanently switched to the [Kaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba) in [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca), the *[qibla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qibla)* (direction of [prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salat)) for Muslims was Jerusalem.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] The city's lasting place in Islam, however, is primarily due to [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad)'s [Night Journey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isra_and_Mi'raj) (c. 620 CE). Muslims believe that Muhammad was miraculously transported one night from the [Great Mosque of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Mecca) to the Temple Mount in Jerusalem, whereupon he ascended to [Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jannah) to meet previous [prophets of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_of_Islam).[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] The first verse in the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qur'an)'s [*Surat al-Isra*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Isra) notes the destination of Muhammad's journey as *al-masjid al-aqṣā* ("the farthest place of prayer").[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] In the earliest days of Islam, this was understood as a reference to a site in the heavens,[^cite_note-283] however, Post-[Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) Islamic scholars understood it as relating to Jerusalem, and particularly to the site of the former Jewish Temple.[^cite_note-284] The [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith), a collection of the sayings of Muhammad, mentions that the location of the Al-Aqsa Mosque (Al-Masjid Al-Aqsa) is in Jerusalem.[^cite_note-285] According to Islamic tradition, the entire site on Temple Mount is [Al-Masjid Al-Aqsa, also called Al-Aqsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Aqsa) or Al-Aqsa Mosque Compound, and not merely the mosque located at its southern end, which is currently named the [Al-Aqsa Mosque or Qibli Mosque](65759.md),[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287] The first erected [congregational mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregational_mosque) in the Al-Aqsa Mosque compound have been built by the Rashidun [Caliph Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar) following was the conquest of Jerusalem in 637–38,[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] The mosque was described by the pilgrim [Arculf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arculf), who toured the Holy Land around 670 CE, as having a capacity of approximately three thousand worshippers.[^cite_note-\:1-290] The first [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) monument to be built on the compound is [the Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock) by [Caliph Abd al-Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Malik_ibn_Marwan) between 685–691.[^cite_note-\:1-290] He and his son [Al-Walid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Walid_I) (r. 705–715) have also rebuilt the congregational Qibli Mosque between 705–710.[^cite_note-291]

## Economy



[Bank of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Israel)

Historically, Jerusalem's economy was supported almost exclusively by religious pilgrims, as it was far from the major ports of [Jaffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa) and [Gaza](55387.md).[^cite_note-Dumper-1996-292] Jerusalem's religious and cultural landmarks today remain the top draw for foreign visitors, with the majority of tourists visiting the Western Wall and the [Old City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City_\(Jerusalem\)).[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221] In 2010, Jerusalem was named the top leisure travel city in Africa and the Middle East by [*Travel + Leisure magazine*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_+_Leisure).[^cite_note-293] In 2013, 75% of the 3.5 million tourists to Israel visited Jerusalem.[^cite_note-294]



[Har Hotzvim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Har_Hotzvim) high-tech park

Since the establishment of the State of Israel, the national government has remained a major player in Jerusalem's economy. The government, centred in Jerusalem, generates a large number of jobs, and offers subsidies and incentives for new business initiatives and start-ups.[^cite_note-Dumper-1996-292] Although Tel Aviv remains Israel's financial centre, a growing number of [high tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_tech) companies are moving to Jerusalem, providing 12,000 jobs in 2006.[^cite_note-295] Northern Jerusalem's [Har Hotzvim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Har_Hotzvim) industrial park and the [Jerusalem Technology Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Technology_Park) in south Jerusalem are home to large [Research and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_and_Development) centres of international tech companies, among them [Intel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intel_Corporation), [Cisco Systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisco_Systems), [Teva Pharmaceutical Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teva_Pharmaceutical_Industries), [IBM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBM), [Mobileye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobileye), [Johnson & Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_&_Johnson), [Medtronic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medtronic) and more.[^cite_note-296] In April 2015 *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* magazine picked Jerusalem as one of the five emerging tech hubs in the world, proclaiming that "The city has become a flourishing centre for biomed, cleantech, Internet/mobile startups, accelerators, investors and supporting service providers."[^cite_note-297]



[Mamilla Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamilla_Mall) adorned with upscale shops stands just outside the Old City Walls.



[Malha Mall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malha_Mall)

Higher than average percentages are employed in education (17.9% vs. 12.7%); health and welfare (12.6% vs. 10.7%); community and social services (6.4% vs. 4.7%); hotels and restaurants (6.1% vs. 4.7%); and public administration (8.2% vs. 4.7%).[^cite_note-298] During the [British Mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine), a law was passed requiring all buildings to be constructed of [Jerusalem stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_stone) in order to preserve the unique historic and aesthetic character of the city.[^cite_note-299] Complementing this building code, which is still in force, is the discouragement of [heavy industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_industry) in Jerusalem; only about 2.2% of Jerusalem's land is zoned for "industry and infrastructure". By comparison, the percentage of land in Tel Aviv zoned for industry and infrastructure is twice as high, and in Haifa, seven times as high.[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221] Only 8.5% of the [Jerusalem District](844333.md) work force is employed in the manufacturing sector, which is half the national average (15.8%).

Although many statistics indicate economic growth in the city, since 1967, East Jerusalem has lagged behind the development of West Jerusalem.[^cite_note-Dumper-1996-292] Nevertheless, the percentage of households with employed persons is higher for Arab households (76.1%) than for Jewish households (66.8%). The unemployment rate in Jerusalem (8.3%) is slightly better than the national average (9.0%), although the civilian [labour force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_force) accounted for less than half of all persons fifteen years or older—lower in comparison to that of Tel Aviv (58.0%) and [Haifa](55606.md) (52.4%).[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221] Poverty remains a problem in the city as 37% of the families in Jerusalem lived in 2011 below the poverty line. According to a report by the [Association for Civil Rights in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Civil_Rights_in_Israel) (ACRI), 78% of Arabs in Jerusalem lived in poverty in 2012, up from 64% in 2006. While the ACRI attributes the increase to the lack of employment opportunities, infrastructure and a worsening educational system, the activist group [Ir Amim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ir_Amim) blames the legal status of Palestinians in Jerusalem.[^cite_note-300]

The increasing number of educated Palestinians in Jerusalem has brought about positive economic changes.[^cite_note-301] Through reforms and initiatives in sectors like technology, tourism, trade, and infrastructure, they have helped drive economic growth, create jobs, and improve living conditions in the city.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] Various joint summits between Israeli and Palestinian entrepreneurs have been held in the city.[^cite_note-304] [Palestine Investment Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_Investment_Fund) have proposed various projects in Jerusalem.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] Palestinian industrialist [Bashar Masri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashar_Masri) sought to make heavy investments in the city.[^cite_note-307] PA controlled industrial areas are located outskirts of Jerusalem, primarily in [Bir Nabala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bir_Nabala), [Abu Dis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dis) and [Eizariya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eizariya), engaging in manufacture of tyres, food products and concretes.[^cite_note-308]

High-tech industry is an emerging sector within Palestinian society in Jerusalem.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] In 2023, Israel opened a technology park in East Jerusalem, known as EasTech. Local Palestinian engineers are employed in the complex by multinational companies, some of which includes [AT\&T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AT\&T), Natural Intelligence, [Nvidia](39120.md), [Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_\(game_engine\)), and [Synamedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synamedia). Station J, an innovation hub located in Sheikh Jarrah, is another tech hub for Palestinians in the city. Hani Alami, a Jerusalem-based Palestinian entrepreneur, has set up a startup accelerator.[^cite_note-311] As part of [Israeli–Palestinian economic peace efforts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_economic_peace_efforts), interactions between Israeli and Palestinian business communities also contribute to the growth of the Palestinian IT sector in the city.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]

## Urban structure

### High-rise construction

Jerusalem has traditionally had a low-rise skyline. About 18 tall buildings were built at different times in the downtown area when there was no clear policy over the matter. One of them, Holyland Tower 1, Jerusalem's tallest building, is a [skyscraper](29485.md) by international standards, rising 32 stories. Holyland Tower 2, which has been approved for construction, will reach the same height.[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]



Holyland Tower, Jerusalem's tallest building

A new master plan for the city will see many high-rise buildings, including skyscrapers, built in certain, designated areas of downtown Jerusalem. Under the plan, towers will line [Jaffa Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_Road) and [King George Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_George_Street_\(Jerusalem\)). One of the proposed towers along King George Street, the Migdal Merkaz HaYekum, is planned as a 65-story building, which would make it one of the tallest buildings in Israel. At the entrance to the city, near the [Jerusalem Chords Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Chords_Bridge) and the [Central Bus Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Central_Bus_Station), twelve towers rising between 24 and 33 stories will be built, as part of a complex that will also include an open square and an [underground train station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Binyanei_HaUma_Railway_Station) serving a new express line between Jerusalem and Tel Aviv, and will be connected by bridges and tunnels. Eleven of the skyscrapers will be either office or apartment buildings, and one will be a 2,000-room hotel. The complex is expected to attract many businesses from Tel Aviv, and become the city's main business hub. In addition, a complex for the city's courts and the prosecutor's office will be built, as well as new buildings for Central Zionist Archives and [Israel State Archives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_State_Archives).[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] The skyscrapers built throughout the city are expected to contain public space, shops, restaurants, and entertainment venues, and it has been speculated that this may lead to a revitalisation of downtown Jerusalem.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320] In August 2015, the city council approved construction of a 344-foot pyramid-shaped skyscraper designed by [Daniel Libeskind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Libeskind) and Yigal Levi, in place of a rejected previous design by Libeskind; it was set to break ground by 2019.[^cite_note-321]

### New projects in Jerusalem

In 2021 [Bashar Masri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashar_Masri) announced and launched "Lana", a massive mix-used project in [East Jerusalem](343508.md), which is located in the neighbourhood of [Beit Hanina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Hanina). The project is in a partnership between Massar International and the [Orthodox Church of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Church_of_Jerusalem). It features 400 residential apartments along with a vibrant commercial centre that hosts well-known global brands, cinemas, restaurants, cafés and offices. The project also includes modern educational facilities, such as a school and a [kindergarten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kindergarten), catering to the needs of residents. In addition to its focus on residential and commercial aspects, the Lana project emphasises the improvement of infrastructure within the project and its surroundings. This involves the construction of three to four floors of underground parking to accommodate the residents' vehicles conveniently. Furthermore, there is a comprehensive plan to expand the road network surrounding the project, ensuring smooth transportation and accessibility for both residents and visitors. It is situated just 15 minutes away from the historic [Old City of Jerusalem](302972.md).[^cite_note-322]

## Transportation

Main article: [Transport in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Jerusalem)

### Public transport



[Jerusalem Chords Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Chords_Bridge)

Jerusalem is served by highly developed communication infrastructures, making it a leading logistics hub for Israel.

The [Jerusalem Central Bus Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Central_Bus_Station), located on [Jaffa Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_Road), is the busiest bus station in Israel. It is served by [Egged Bus Cooperative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egged_\(company\)), which is the second-largest bus company in the world,[^cite_note-323] The [Dan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Bus_Company) serves the [Bnei Brak](314671.md)-Jerusalem route along with Egged, and [Superbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superbus_\(company\)) serves the routes between Jerusalem, [Modi'in Illit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modi'in_Illit) and [Modi'in-Maccabim-Re'ut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modi'in-Maccabim-Re'ut). The companies operate from Jerusalem Central Bus Station. Arab neighbourhoods in East Jerusalem and routes between Jerusalem and locations in the [West Bank](33209.md) are served by the [East Jerusalem Central Bus Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Jerusalem_Central_Bus_Station), a transportation hub located near the Old City's [Damascus Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damascus_Gate).

### Railway

The [Jerusalem Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Light_Rail) initiated service in August 2011. According to plans, the first rail line will be capable of transporting an estimated 200,000 people daily, and has 23 stops. The route is from Pisgat Ze'ev in the north via the Old City and city centre to Mount Herzl in the south.



[Light Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Light_Rail) tram on [Jaffa Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_Road)

A [high-speed rail line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_railway_to_Jerusalem) connecting Tel Aviv to Jerusalem became partially operational in 2018 and was completed in 2019.[^cite_note-324] Its terminus is at the [new underground station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Binyanei_HaUma_Railway_Station) (80 m or 262 ft deep) serving the [International Convention Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Convention_Center_\(Jerusalem\)) and the Central Bus Station,[^cite_note-325] and is planned to be extended eventually to [Malha station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Malha_Railway_Station). [Israel Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Railways) operated train services to Malha train station from Tel Aviv via [Beit Shemesh](323292.md), but this service was discontinued in 2020.[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328]

[Begin Expressway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highway_50_\(Israel\)) is one of Jerusalem's major north–south thoroughfares; it runs on the western side of the city, merging in the north with [Route 443](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Route_443_\(Israel\)), which continues toward Tel Aviv. [Route 60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highway_60_\(Israel\)) runs through the centre of the city near the [Green Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Line_\(Israel\)) between East and West Jerusalem. Construction is progressing on parts of a 35 km (22 mi) [ring road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beltway) around the city, fostering faster connection between the suburbs.[^cite_note-Burstein-2006-329][^cite_note-330] The eastern half of the project was conceptualised decades ago, but reaction to the proposed highway is still mixed.[^cite_note-Burstein-2006-329]

### Airport

In the past, Jerusalem was also served by the local [Jerusalem International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atarot_Airport), locally known as Atarot Airport. It was the first airport built in the British Mandate of Palestine. Palestinians considered the Atarot Airport as a "symbol of Palestinian sovereignty".[^cite_note-331] The airport falls beyond Green Line. After the 1948 war it came under control of Jordan. Following the Six Day War of 1967, the airport came under control of Israel.[^cite_note-332] With increase of violence in the [second intifada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_intifada), Atarot Airport ceased operation in 2000. Today Jerusalem is served by [Ben Gurion Airport](113149.md), some 50 km (30 mi) northwest of the Jerusalem, on the route to Tel Aviv. The [Tel Aviv–Jerusalem railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv%E2%80%93Jerusalem_railway) runs non-stop from [Jerusalem–Yitzhak Navon railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem%E2%80%93Yitzhak_Navon_railway_station) to the airport and began operation in 2018.[^cite_note-333]

The Australian businessman [Kevin Bermeister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Bermeister) proposed a masterplan of Jerusalem, which also includes the development of an airport for Jerusalem in the [Jordan Valley](12105764.md), near [Jericho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jericho).[^cite_note-334] The airport is sought to be a joint Israeli-Palestinian airport. Palestinian Prime Minister [Mohammad Shtayyeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Shtayyeh) have also appealed to Israeli authorities to redevelop the airport.[^cite_note-335] In 2021, the Israeli government planned to redevelop Atarot Airport as a joint Israeli–Palestinian airport.[^cite_note-336] The new Atarot Airport will include two separate Israeli and Palestinian terminals.

## Education

### Universities

Jerusalem is home to several prestigious universities offering courses in [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) and English.



[Hebrew University of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_University_of_Jerusalem), [Mount Scopus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Scopus) campus

&#x20;Founded in 1925, the [Hebrew University of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_University_of_Jerusalem) has been ranked among the top 100 schools in the world.[^cite_note-337] The Board of Governors has included such prominent Jewish intellectuals as [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein) and [Sigmund Freud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmund_Freud).[^cite_note-338] The university has produced several [Nobel](21201.md) laureates; recent winners associated with Hebrew University include [Avram Hershko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avram_Hershko),[^cite_note-339] [David Gross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Gross),[^cite_note-340] and [Daniel Kahneman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Kahneman).[^cite_note-341] One of the university's major assets is the [Jewish National and University Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Israel), which houses over five million books.[^cite_note-342] The library opened in 1892, over three decades before the university was established, and is one of the world's largest repositories of books on Jewish subjects. Today it is both the central library of the university and the national library of Israel.[^cite_note-343] The Hebrew University operates three campuses in Jerusalem, on Mount Scopus, on [Giv'at Ram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Givat_Ram) and a medical campus at the [Hadassah Ein Kerem hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadassah_Medical_Center). The [Academy of the Hebrew Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_of_the_Hebrew_Language) are located in the Hebrew university in Givat Ram and the [Israel Academy of Sciences and Humanities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Academy_of_Sciences_and_Humanities) located near the [Presidents House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_HaNassi).



[Hebron Yeshiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebron_Yeshiva) in [Givat Mordechai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Givat_Mordechai) neighbourhood

The [Jerusalem College of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_College_of_Technology), founded in 1969, combines training in engineering and other high-tech industries with a Jewish studies programme.[^cite_note-344] It is one of many schools in Jerusalem, from elementary school and up, that combine secular and religious studies. Numerous religious educational institutions and [*Yeshivot*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeshiva), including some of the most prestigious yeshivas, among them the [Brisk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brisk_yeshiva), [Chevron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_yeshiva), [Midrash Shmuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midrash_Shmuel_Yeshiva) and [Mir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir_yeshiva_\(Jerusalem\)), are based in the city, with the Mir Yeshiva claiming to be the largest.[^cite_note-345] There were nearly 8,000 twelfth-grade students in Hebrew-language schools during the 2003–2004 school year.[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221] However, due to the large portion of students in [Haredi Jewish](53835.md) frameworks, only fifty-five percent of twelfth graders took [matriculation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matriculation) exams (*[Bagrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrut)*) and only thirty-seven percent were eligible to graduate. Unlike [public schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_school_\(government_funded\)), many Haredi schools do not prepare students to take standardised tests.[^cite_note-Central_Bureau_of_Statistics-2006-221] To attract more university students to Jerusalem, the city has begun to offer a special package of financial incentives and housing subsidies to students who rent apartments in downtown Jerusalem.[^cite_note-346]



Inside [Abu Jihad Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Jihad) of [Al-Quds University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Quds_University)&#x20;

[Al-Quds University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Quds_University) was established in 1984[^cite_note-al-Quds_University-347] to serve as a flagship university for the Arab and Palestinian peoples. It describes itself as the "only Arab university in Jerusalem".[^cite_note-348] [Bard College](276259.md) of Annandale-on-Hudson, New York and Al-Quds University agreed to open a joint college in a building originally built to house the [Palestinian Legislative Council](40067464.md) and [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat)'s office. The college gives [Master of Arts in Teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_of_Arts_in_Teaching) degrees.[^cite_note-349] Al-Quds University resides southeast of the city proper on a 190,000 m<sup>2</sup> (47-acre) [Abu Dis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dis) campus.[^cite_note-al-Quds_University-347] Other campuses of AQU are located within city limits of Jerusalem. A campus of university in [Sheikh Jarrah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh_Jarrah), which is one of the oldest faculties, is known as Hind Al Husseini College for Arts.[^cite_note-350] It was named after [Hind al-Husseini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hind_al-Husseini), a Palestinian activists known for rescuing orphaned survivors of [Deir Yassin massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_Yassin_massacre) and giving them shelter in a palace of her grandfather, which was converted into an orphanage and later a college, which is a part today's Al Quds University.[^cite_note-351] A joint campus of AQU and Bard College is located in [Beit Hanina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Hanina). [Bayt Mal Al Qods Acharif Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayt_Mal_Al_Qods_Acharif_Agency), a Moroccan organisation, is constructing a new campus in the same neighbourhood.[^cite_note-352]

Other institutions of higher learning in Jerusalem are the [Jerusalem Academy of Music and Dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Academy_of_Music_and_Dance)[^cite_note-353] and [Bezalel Academy of Art and Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bezalel_Academy_of_Arts_and_Design),[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355] whose buildings are located on the campuses of the Hebrew University.

### Arab schools



[Hand in Hand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_in_Hand:_Center_for_Jewish-Arab_Education_in_Israel), a bilingual Jewish-Arab school in Jerusalem

Israel's public schools for Arabs in Jerusalem and other parts of the country have been criticised for offering a lower quality education than those catering to Israeli Jewish students.[^cite_note-Second_Class_Discrimination_Against_Palestinian_Arab_Children_in_Israel's_Schools-2001-356] While many schools in the heavily Arab East Jerusalem are filled to capacity and there have been complaints of overcrowding, the Jerusalem Municipality is building over a dozen new schools in the city's Arab neighbourhoods.[^cite_note-357] Schools in [Ras el-Amud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ras_al-Amud) and [Umm Lison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Lison?action=edit\&redlink=1) opened in 2008.[^cite_note-Lis-2008-358] In March 2007 the Israeli government approved a five-year plan to build 8,000 new classrooms in the city, 40 percent in the Arab sector and 28 percent in the Haredi sector. A budget of 4.6 billion shekels was allocated for this project.[^cite_note-359] In 2008, Jewish British philanthropists donated \$3 million for the construction of schools for Arabs in East Jerusalem.[^cite_note-Lis-2008-358] Arab high school students take the *[Bagrut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagrut)* matriculation exams, so that much of their curriculum parallels that of other Israeli high schools and includes certain Jewish subjects.[^cite_note-Second_Class_Discrimination_Against_Palestinian_Arab_Children_in_Israel's_Schools-2001-356]

## Culture



The [Shrine of the Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_the_Book), housing the [Dead Sea Scrolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Sea_Scrolls), at the [Israel Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Museum)

Although Jerusalem is known primarily for its [religious significance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_significance_of_Jerusalem), the city is also home to many artistic and cultural venues. The [Israel Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Museum) attracts nearly one million visitors a year, approximately one-third of them tourists.[^cite_note-The_Israel_Museum,_Jerusalem-360] The 8 ha (20-acre) museum complex comprises several buildings featuring special exhibits and extensive collections of [Judaica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_ceremonial_art), archaeological findings, and Israeli and European art. The [Dead Sea scrolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Sea_scrolls), discovered in the mid-20th century in the [Qumran Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qumran_Caves) near the Dead Sea, are housed in the Museum's [Shrine of the Book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrine_of_the_Book).[^cite_note-361] The Youth Wing, which mounts changing exhibits and runs an extensive art education programme, is visited by 100,000 children a year. The museum has a large outdoor sculpture garden and includes the [Holyland Model of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holyland_Model_of_Jerusalem), a scale-model of the city during the late [Second Temple period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_period).[^cite_note-The_Israel_Museum,_Jerusalem-360] The [Ticho House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticho_House) in downtown Jerusalem houses the paintings of [Anna Ticho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Ticho) and the Judaica collections of her husband, an ophthalmologist who opened Jerusalem's first eye clinic in this building in 1912.[^cite_note-362]



[Jerusalem Biblical Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Biblical_Zoo)

Next to the Israel Museum is the [Bible Lands Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible_Lands_Museum), near [The National Campus for the Archaeology of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Antiquities_Authority#The_National_Campus_for_the_Archaeology_of_Israel), which includes the [Israel Antiquities Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Antiquities_Authority) offices. A World Bible Centre is planned to be built adjacent to [Mount Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Zion) at a site called the "Bible Hill". A planned [World Kabbalah Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Kabbalah_Center?action=edit\&redlink=1) is to be located on the nearby [promenade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armon_Hanatziv_Promenade), overlooking the Old City. The [Rockefeller Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockefeller_Museum), located in East Jerusalem, was the first archaeological museum in the Middle East. It was built in 1938 during the British Mandate.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364] In 2006, a 38 km (24 mi) [Jerusalem Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Trail) was opened, a hiking trail that goes to many cultural sites and [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_parks) in and around Jerusalem. The [Jerusalem Biblical Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Biblical_Zoo) has ranked consistently as Israel's top tourist attraction for Israelis.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] The national cemetery of Israel is located at the city's western edge, near the [Jerusalem Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Forest) on [Mount Herzl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Herzl). The western extension of Mount Herzl is the Mount of Remembrance, where the main Holocaust museum of Israel is located. [Yad Vashem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yad_Vashem), Israel's national memorial to the victims of the [Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust), houses the world's largest library of Holocaust-related information.[^cite_note-367] It houses an estimated 100,000 books and articles. The complex contains a state-of-the-art museum that explores the genocide of the Jews through exhibits that focus on the personal stories of individuals and families killed in the Holocaust. An art gallery featuring the work of artists who perished is also present. Further, Yad Vashem commemorates the 1.5 million Jewish children murdered by the [Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazis), and honours the [Righteous among the Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Righteous_among_the_Nations).[^cite_note-368]



The new building of the [National Library of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Israel)

The [Jerusalem Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Symphony_Orchestra), established in the 1940s,[^cite_note-Jerusalem_Orchestra-369] has appeared around the world.[^cite_note-Jerusalem_Orchestra-369] The [International Convention Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Convention_Center_\(Jerusalem\)) (*Binyanei HaUma*) near the entrance to city houses the [Israel Philharmonic Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Philharmonic_Orchestra). The Jerusalem Cinemateque, the [Gerard Behar Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_Behar_Center) (formerly Beit Ha'Am) in downtown Jerusalem, the [Jerusalem Music Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Music_Centre) in [Yemin Moshe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemin_Moshe),[^cite_note-370] and the Targ Music Centre in [Ein Kerem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ein_Kerem) also present the arts. The [Israel Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Festival), featuring indoor and outdoor performances by local and international singers, concerts, plays, and street theatre has been held annually since 1961, and Jerusalem has been the major organiser of this event. The [Jerusalem Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Theater) in the [Talbiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talbiya) neighbourhood hosts over 150 concerts a year, as well as theatre and dance companies and performing artists from overseas.[^cite_note-371] The [Khan Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jerusalem_Khan_Theatre), located in a caravanserai opposite the old Jerusalem train station, is the city's only [repertoire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repertory) theatre.[^cite_note-372] The station itself has become a venue for cultural events in recent years as the site of *Shav'ua Hasefer* (an annual week-long book fair) and outdoor music performances.[^cite_note-373] The [Jerusalem Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Film_Festival) is held annually, screening Israeli and international films.[^cite_note-374] In 1974 the [Jerusalem Cinematheque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Cinematheque) was founded. In 1981 it was moved to a new building on Hebron Road near the [Valley of Hinnom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley_of_Hinnom) and the Old City.

Jerusalem was declared the [Capital of Arab Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Capital_of_Arab_Culture) in 2009.[^cite_note-Ynetnews-1995-375] Jerusalem is home to the [Palestinian National Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Theatre), which engages in cultural preservation as well as innovation, working to rekindle Palestinian interest in the arts.[^cite_note-376] [The Edward Said National Conservatory of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Edward_Said_National_Conservatory_of_Music) sponsors the Palestine Youth Orchestra[^cite_note-377] which toured [Arab states of the Persian Gulf](3088555.md) and other Middle East countries in 2009.[^cite_note-378] The [Islamic Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Museum) on the Temple Mount, established in 1923, houses many Islamic artefacts, from tiny [kohl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kohl_\(cosmetics\)) flasks and rare manuscripts to giant marble columns.[^cite_note-379] Al-Hoash, established in 2004, is a gallery for the preservation of Palestinian art.[^cite_note-380] While Israel approves and financially supports some Arab cultural activities,[^cite_note-381] Arab Capital of Culture events were banned because they were sponsored by the [Palestine National Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_National_Authority).[^cite_note-Ynetnews-1995-375] In 2009 a four-day culture festival was held in the [Beit 'Anan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_'Anan) suburb of Jerusalem, attended by more than 15,000 people[^cite_note-382]

[Palestinian cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_cinema) is based in the city.[^cite_note-383] Jerusalem has been location for "Jerusalem Arab Film Festival", for exhibiting [Palestinian films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Palestine).[^cite_note-384] The city is home to numerous artists, singers, actors, actresses and filmmakers.[^cite_note-385] Established in 1991, [Riwaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riwaq_\(organization\)) have been working on various projects to restore cultural and historical sites across [Palestine](241405.md).[^cite_note-386] Difficulties to operate in the [annexed areas of Palestinian Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_annexation_of_East_Jerusalem), it have successfully worked across those neighbourhoods, rural and suburban area and Jerusalem Mountains (*Jibal al-Quds*), where the [Palestinian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_government) has control.[^cite_note-387] So far, the organisation have restored a number of sites across the neighbourhoods of [Kafr 'Aqab](18080956.md), [Al Jib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Jib), [Jaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaba',_Jerusalem) and [Qalandia](400893.md).[^cite_note-tadamon.community-388] Those restored structures serves as local community centres, cultural sites and headquarters of several NGOs and cultural groups.[^cite_note-tadamon.community-388] Yabous Cultural Center is the largest cultural centre in the city, opened by Palestinian groups in 1997.[^cite_note-389] [Edward Said National Conservatory of Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Said_National_Conservatory_of_Music) have a branch in Jerusalem.

[Hadassah Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadassah_Medical_Center) and the Hebrew University have unveiled a "Tree of Peace" statue at the [Al Quds University School of Dental Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Quds_University_Teaching_Hospital).[^cite_note-390] The Museum on the Seam, which explores issues of coexistence through art, is situated on the road dividing eastern and western Jerusalem.[^cite_note-391] The Abraham Fund and the Jerusalem Intercultural Centre (JICC) promote joint Jewish-Palestinian cultural projects. The Jerusalem Centre for Middle Eastern Music and Dance[^cite_note-392] is open to Arabs and Jews and offers workshops on Jewish-Arab dialogue through the arts.[^cite_note-393] The Jewish-Arab Youth Orchestra performs both European classical and Middle Eastern music.[^cite_note-394] In 2008 the [Tolerance Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tolerance_Monument), an outdoor sculpture by [Czesław Dźwigaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czes%C5%82aw_D%C5%BAwigaj), was erected on a hill between Jewish [Armon HaNetziv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Talpiot) and Arab [Jebl Mukaber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabel_Mukaber) as a symbol of Jerusalem's quest for peace.[^cite_note-395]

### Media

The headquarters of the [Israel Broadcasting Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Broadcasting_Authority) and its successor [Israeli Public Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Public_Broadcasting_Corporation) are located in Jerusalem, as well as television and radio studios for [Channel 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_12_\(Israel\)), [Channel 13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_13_\(Israel\)), and part of the radio studios of [BBC News](1139893.md). *[The Jerusalem Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jerusalem_Post)* and *[The Times of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_of_Israel)* are also headquartered in Jerusalem. Local newspapers include the Israeli *[Kol Ha'ir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kol_Ha'ir)* and the Palestinian [*Jerusalem Times*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jerusalem_Times). [God TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GOD_TV), an international Christian television network, is also based in the city. [PYALARA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Youth_Association_for_Leadership_and_Rights_Activation), an organisation based in Jerusalem, transformed [Jaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaba',_Jerusalem) into a digital hub, which is the Middle East's first digital village and is also home to the first Media Interactive Learning Center in the Middle East.[^cite_note-396]

### Sports

See also: [Beitar Jerusalem F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beitar_Jerusalem_F.C.), [Hapoel Jerusalem B.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapoel_Jerusalem_B.C.), and [Jerusalem Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Marathon)



[Teddy Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teddy_Stadium), [Malha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malha)



[Pais Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pais_Arena)

The two most popular sports are [football](10568.md) (soccer) and basketball.[^cite_note-397] [Beitar Jerusalem Football Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beitar_Jerusalem_F.C.) is one of the most well known in Israel. Fans include political figures who often attend its games.[^cite_note-398] Jerusalem's other major football team, and one of Beitar's top rivals, is [Hapoel Jerusalem F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapoel_Jerusalem_F.C.) Whereas Beitar has been [Israel State Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_State_Cup) champion seven times,[^cite_note-399] Hapoel has won the Cup only once. Beitar has won the top league six times, while Hapoel has never succeeded. Beitar plays in the more prestigious [Ligat HaAl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Premier_League), while Hapoel is in the second division [Liga Leumit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liga_Leumit). Since its opening in 1992, [Teddy Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teddy_Stadium) has been Jerusalem's primary football stadium, with a capacity of 31,733.[^cite_note-400]

The most popular Palestinian football club is [Jabal Al Mukaber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_Al_Mukaber_\(football_club\)) (since 1976) which plays in West Bank Premier League. The club hails from Mount Scopus at Jerusalem, part of the [Asian Football Confederation](715249.md), and plays at the [Faisal Al-Husseini International Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_Al-Husseini_International_Stadium) at [Al-Ram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ram), across the [West Bank Barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Bank_Barrier).[^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402]

In basketball [Hapoel Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapoel_Jerusalem_B.C.) is one of the top teams in the [top division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Basketball_Super_League). The club has won Israel's championship in 2015, the [State Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Basketball_State_Cup) four times, and the [ULEB Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroCup_Basketball) in 2004.[^cite_note-403]

The [Jerusalem Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Marathon), established in 2011, is an international marathon race held annually in Jerusalem in the month of March. The full 42-kilometre race begins at the Knesset, passes through Mount Scopus and the Old City's Armenian Quarter, and concludes at Sacher Park. In 2012, the Jerusalem Marathon drew 15,000 runners, including 1,500 from fifty countries outside Israel.[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408]

A popular non-competitive sports event is the [Jerusalem March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_March),[^cite_note-409] held annually during the [Sukkot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukkot) festival.

  [^cite_note-421]

[^cite_note-4]: &#x20;The State of Palestine (according to the Basic Law of Palestine, Title One: Article 3) regards Jerusalem as its capital.<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-2003_Amended_Basic_Law_Archived_11_February_2016_at_the_Wayback_Machine-1)</sup> However, the documents of the [PLO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PLO)'s Negotiations Affairs Department (NAD) often refer to [East Jerusalem](343508.md) (rather than the whole of Jerusalem) as a future capital, and sometimes as the current capital. One of its 2010 documents, described as "for discussion purposes only", says that Palestine has a "vision" for a future in which "East Jerusalem \[...] shall be the capital of Palestine, and West Jerusalem shall be the capital of Israel."<sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-2)</sup> One of its 2013 documents refers to "Palestine's capital, East Jerusalem", and states that "Occupied East Jerusalem is the natural socio-economic and political center for the future Palestinian state", while also stating that "Jerusalem has always been and remains the political, administrative and spiritual heart of Palestine" and that "The Palestinian acceptance of the 1967 border, which includes East Jerusalem, is a painful compromise".<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-3)</sup>

[^cite_note-10]: &#x20;Jerusalem is the capital under [Israeli law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_Law). The presidential residence, government offices, supreme court and parliament ([Knesset](74656.md)) are there. The State of Palestine (according to the Basic Law of Palestine, Title One: Article 3) regards Jerusalem as its capital.<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-2003_Amended_Basic_Law_Archived_11_February_2016_at_the_Wayback_Machine-1)</sup> The UN and most countries do not recognise Jerusalem as Israel's capital, taking the position that the final status of Jerusalem is pending future negotiations between Israel and the Palestinian Authority. Most countries maintain their embassies in [Tel Aviv](31453.md) and its suburbs or suburbs of Jerusalem, such as [Mevaseret Zion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mevaseret_Zion) (see ["Map of Israel"](https://www.un.org/Depts/Cartographic/map/profile/israel.pdf) (PDF). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200601135924/https://www.un.org/Depts/Cartographic/map/profile/israel.pdf) (PDF) from the original on 1 June 2020. Retrieved 28 June 2017. (319 KB)) See [Status of Jerusalem](4024423.md) for more information.

[^cite_note-Teller-2022-21]: &#x20;Teller, Matthew (2022). [*Nine Quarters of Jerusalem: A New Biography of the Old City*](https://books.google.com/books?id=SgQ3EAAAQBAJ). [Profile Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profile_Books). p. Chapter 1. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-78283-904-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-78283-904-0). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230609194835/https://books.google.com/books?id=SgQ3EAAAQBAJ) from the original on 9 June 2023. Retrieved 30 May 2023. What wasn't corrected, though—and what, in retrospect, should have raised much more controversy than it did (it seems to have passed completely unremarked for the last 170-odd years)—was [\[Aldrich and Symonds's\] map's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1840%E2%80%9341_Royal_Engineers_maps_of_Palestine,_Lebanon_and_Syria) labelling. Because here, newly arcing across the familiar quadrilateral of Jerusalem, are four double labels in bold capitals. At top left *Haret En-Nassara* and, beneath it, *Christian Quarter*; at bottom left *Haret El-Arman* and *Armenian Quarter*; at bottom centre *Haret El-Yehud* and *Jews' Quarter*; and at top right—the big innovation, covering perhaps half the city—*Haret El-Muslimin* and *Mohammedan Quarter*, had shown this before. Every map has shown it since. The idea, in 1841, of a Mohammedan (that is, Muslim) quarter of Jerusalem is bizarre. It's like a Catholic quarter of Rome. A Hindu quarter of Delhi. Nobody living there would conceive of the city in such a way. At that time, and for centuries before and decades after, Jerusalem was, if the term means anything at all, a Muslim city. Many people identified in other ways, but large numbers of Jerusalemites were Muslim and they lived all over the city. A Muslim quarter could only have been dreamt up by outsiders, searching for a handle on a place they barely understood, intent on asserting their own legitimacy among a hostile population, seeing what they wanted to see. Its only purpose could be to draw attention to what it excludes.

[^cite_note-30]: &#x20;Mark Smith in "The Early History of God: Yahweh and Other Deities of Ancient Israel" states "Despite the long regnant model that the Canaanites and Israelites were people of fundamentally different culture, archaeological data now casts doubt on this view. The material culture of the region exhibits numerous common points between Israelites and Canaanites in the Iron I period (c. 1200–1000 BCE). The record would suggest that the Israelite culture largely overlapped with and derived from Canaanite culture... In short, Israelite culture was largely Canaanite in nature. Given the information available, one cannot maintain a radical cultural separation between Canaanites and Israelites for the Iron I period." (pp. 6–7). Accessed June 7, 2026. "Two events in the life of Abraham place him in close proximity to Jerusalem. Melchizedek, the king of Salem (Gen 14\:18; cf. Ps 76\:2), met Abram after his rescue of Lot. Later Abraham took his son Isaac to one of the mountains in the 'region of Moriah' to sacrifice him (Gen 22\:2), the same place where Solomon built the temple (2 Chron 3\:1). Jerusalem next appears in the stories of the conquest under Joshua. When the king of Jerusalem, Adoni-Zedek, heard of the Gibeonites’ treaty with Joshua, he realized that his major line of communication with the coast, and hence to Egypt, was in jeopardy. He assembled a coalition of four other Amorite kings and attacked Gibeon but was defeated by Joshua."

"Written in ancient Hebrew script, and dating back to the Kingdom of Judah during the 7th century BCE, the relic made of papyrus is the earliest extra-biblical source to mention Jerusalem in Hebrew writing....“Most of the letters are clearly legible, and the proposed reading of the text appears as follows: 'From the king's maidservant, from Naharata, jars of wine, to Jerusalem.'"

Accessed June 26, 2026. "A sample of the certificate was sent to the Radiocarbon Dating Laboratory, directed by Dr. Elisabetta Boaretto at the Weizmann Institute in Rehovot (sample no. RTD8062). The results confirmed a date range for the papyrus between the mid-eighth century BCE and the last third of the fifth century BCE, a range that falls within the Hallstatt Plateau, thus making it difficult to date with any greater precision. Palaeographic examination of the text indicates that the certificate should be dated to around the second half of the seventh century BCE, which is compatible with the laboratory results (see below; for a description of palaeographic changes in the seventh–sixth centuries BCE, see Cross 2003b)."

[doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.2307/2203016](https://doi.org/10.2307%2F2203016). [JSTOR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JSTOR_\(identifier\)) [2203016](https://www.jstor.org/stable/2203016). The international community has taken a critical view of both deportations and settlements as being contrary to international law. General Assembly resolutions have condemned the deportations since 1969, and have done so by overwhelming majorities in recent years. Likewise, they have consistently deplored the establishment of settlements, and have done so by overwhelming majorities throughout the period (since the end of 1976) of the rapid expansion in their numbers. The Security Council has also been critical of deportations and settlements; and other bodies have viewed them as an obstacle to peace, and illegal under international law\... Although East Jerusalem and the Golan Heights have been brought directly under Israeli law, by acts that amount to annexation, both of these areas continue to be viewed by the international community as occupied, and their status as regards the applicability of international rules is in most respects identical to that of the West Bank and Gaza.

"The body of Theodor Herzl, who is credited with transforming Zionism from a mystic dream to a practical movement leading to the establishment of the Jewish State, was reinterred here today on the peak of Jerusalem's highest hill. An estimated 400,000 Israelis turned out to see the coffin between its landing yesterday at Lydda Airport and the final interment today."

[National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration](37876.md). Archived from [the original](16043.md) on 19 October 2017. Retrieved 26 April 2017.

[^cite_note-214]: &#x20; ["40183: Jerusalem Centre (Israel)"](https://ogimet.com/cgi-bin/gsynres?ind=40183\&ano=2025\&mes=11\&day=5\&hora=18\&min=0\&ndays=30). *ogimet.com*. OGIMET. 5 November 2025. Retrieved 9 November 2025.

[^cite_note-215]: &#x20;Teller, Matthew (2022). [*Nine Quarters of Jerusalem: A New Biography of the Old City*](https://books.google.com/books?id=SgQ3EAAAQBAJ). [Profile Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profile_Books). p. Chapter 1. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-1-78283-904-0](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-1-78283-904-0). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230609194835/https://books.google.com/books?id=SgQ3EAAAQBAJ) from the original on 9 June 2023. Retrieved 30 May 2023. But it may not have been Aldrich and Symonds. Below the frame of their map, printed in italic script, a single line notes that 'The Writing' had been added by 'the [Revd. G. Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Williams_\(priest\))' and 'the Revd. Robert Willis'… Some sources suggest \[Williams] arrived before [\[Michael\] Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Alexander_\(bishop\)), in 1841. If so, did he meet Aldrich and Symonds? We don't know. But Williams became their champion, defending them when the Haram inaccuracy came up and then publishing their work. The survey the two Royal Engineers did was not intended for commercial release (Aldrich had originally been sent to [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(region\)) under 'secret service'), and it was several years before their military plan of Jerusalem came to public attention, published first in 1845 by their senior officer Alderson in plain form, without most of the detail and labelling, and then in full in 1849, in the second edition of Williams's book The Holy City. Did Aldrich and/or Symonds invent the idea of four quarters in Jerusalem? It's possible, but they were military surveyors, not scholars. It seems more likely they spent their very short stay producing a usable street-plan for their superior officers, without necessarily getting wrapped up in details of names and places. The 1845 publication, shorn of street names, quarter labels and other detail, suggests that… Compounding his anachronisms, and perhaps with an urge to reproduce Roman urban design in this new context, Williams writes how two main streets, north-south and east-west, 'divide Jerusalem into four quarters.' Then the crucial line: 'The subdivisions of the streets and quarters are numerous, but unimportant.' Historians will, I hope, be able to delve more deeply into Williams's work, but for me, this is evidence enough. For almost two hundred years, virtually the entire world has accepted the ill-informed, dismissive judgementalism of a jejune Old Etonian missionary as representing enduring fact about the social make-up of Jerusalem. It's shameful… With Britain's increased standing in Palestine after 1840, and the growth of interest in biblical archaeology that was to become an obsession a few decades later, it was vital for the Protestant missionaries to establish boundaries in Jerusalem… Williams spread his ideas around. [Ernst Gustav Schultz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Gustav_Schultz?action=edit\&redlink=1), who came to Jerusalem in 1842 as Prussian vice-consul, writes in his 1845 book Jerusalem: *Eine Vorlesung* ('A Lecture'): 'It is with sincere gratitude I must mention that, on my arrival in Jerusalem, Mr Williams ... willingly alerted me to the important information that he \[and] another young Anglican clergyman, Mr Rolands, had discovered about the topography of \[Jerusalem].' Later come the lines: 'Let us now divide the city into quarters,' and, after mentioning Jews and Christians, 'All the rest of the city is the Mohammedan Quarter.' Included was [a map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiepert_maps_of_Palestine_and_Jerusalem), drawn by [Heinrich Kiepert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_Kiepert), that labelled the four quarters, mirroring Williams's treatment in *The Holy City*.

"The period known as the Three Weeks (also known in Hebrew as Bein ha-Metzarim – 'Between the Straits/days of distress') is the saddest period in the Jewish calendar. Customs of mourning are observed during this time to commemorate the destruction of the First and Second Temples.... Tisha B'Av: This is the saddest day in the Jewish calendar, when the destruction of both the First and Second Temples is commemorated.... The First Temple, built by King Shlomo, was destroyed by the Babylonian King Nebuchadnezzar in 586 BCE, and the population of the Kingdom of Judah was sent into exile. The Second Temple was destroyed by the Romans in 70 CE, scattering the people of Judea and signifying the beginning of a two-thousand-year exile."

Accessed June 28, 2026. "The Three Weeks, beginning on the 17th of Tammuz and concluding with the 9th of Av (Tisha B’Av), are a solemn period of mourning that commemorates a series of tragic events in Jewish history, primarily the destruction of the First and Second Temples in Jerusalem."

[^cite_note-262]: &#x20;["A House Unfinished"](https://njop.org/a-house-unfinished/), National Jewish Outreach Program, July 31, 2014. Accessed July 15, 2026. "The Hebrew name for the Holy Temple is the Beit Hamikdash, the House of Holiness, a term that invokes a warm sentiment of a place where one is at home. It is therefore, perhaps, appropriate that one of the ways of mourning the loss of the Beit Hamikdash 2,000 years ago is to mark one’s home.... To properly fulfill this custom, one who builds a new house should leave a one-cubit-by-one-cubit (about 18 inches by 18 inches) square of wall without paint, plaster or wall paper.... As per Rabbi Hisda’s comment, the unfinished square is generally created near the entrance to the house, either above or opposite the door."

[^cite_note-263]: &#x20;[Wolpe, David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Wolpe). ["Breaking the glass"](https://blogs.timesofisrael.com/breaking-the-glass/), *[The Times of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_of_Israel)*, July 29, 2021. Accessed May 5, 2026. "Before the groom steps on a glass to conclude the wedding ceremony, there is a tradition of reciting part of the 137th Psalm, 'If I forget thee O Jerusalem….' The most immediate explanation of this is that stepping on the glass commemorates the tragedies of Jewish history, particularly the destruction of the Temple. So as we mark the destruction and exile, we promise not to forget."

[^cite_note-Mourning-264]: &#x20;Grumet, Zvi. ["The Mourners of Zion and Jerusalem"](https://blogs.timesofisrael.com/the-mourners-of-zion-and-jerusalem/), *[The Times of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_of_Israel)*, October 19, 2023. Accessed June 10, 2026. "Ashkenazi Jews have a custom of reciting the following when visiting a shiva: Hamakom yenahem etkhem betokh she’ar aveilei Tzion ViYerushalayim. May the Omnipresent comfort you amongst all the mourners of Zion and Jerusalem."

[^cite_note-265]: &#x20;Moss, Aron. ["Who Are 'The Mourners of Zion and Jerusalem'?"](https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/300864/jewish/Who-Are-The-Mourners-of-Zion-and-Jerusalem.htm), [Chabad.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chabad.org). Accessed June 10, 2026. "I was wondering about the traditional words of consolation said to mourners: 'May the Almighty comfort you amongst the mourners of Zion and Jerusalem.'... After two millennia we still mourn for the loss of Jerusalem, but the Jewish people have never lost hope that Jerusalem will one day be rebuilt. In a similar way, we mourn the loss of our loved ones, but we have faith that we will one day be reunited with them, for our prophets have promised that the dead will come back to life in the messianic era.... While the Romans were able to destroy the buildings of Jerusalem, its spirit and inner holiness were beyond their reach."
